01 Property Tax Comparison — HD109 vs.
Dallas County vs.
Texas City-portion property tax rates, 2017–2025 (taxable-value-weighted averages) $0.621 HD109 Avg City Rate, 2025 (per $100) $0.635 Dallas County Avg, 2025 $0.532 Texas Statewide Avg, 2025 +16.8% HD109 Above State Avg, 2025 Summary of Findings HD109 cities carry a heavier city-tax burden than the statewide average every year from 2017-2025.
The gap has narrowed from 31.1% above the state average in 2017 to 16.8% above in 2025, driven mainly by rate cuts in Lancaster, Glenn Heights, and DeSoto [Texas Comptroller, 2017-2025] .
HD109's rate advantage over Dallas County as a whole flipped in 2023.
HD109 cities were taxed 7.9% above the Dallas County city average in 2017, but by 2023-2025 HD109 cities' average rate fell slightly below the county-wide average, reflecting faster local rate reductions relative to the rest of the county.
Seagoville, DeSoto, and Hutchins carry the highest city tax rates in HD109 as of 2025 (0.721, 0.685, and 0.657 per $100, respectively), while Combine and Wilmer carry the lowest (0.350 and 0.468).

The 2020 file was retrieved via the Internet Archive Wayback Machine after being removed from the Comptroller's live rates page.
A 2016 dataset could not be located on the Comptroller's site or in web archives; the ten-year comparison therefore spans 2017-2025 (nine years of verified primary data), and this gap is disclosed rather than filled with an estimate.
For each year, every Texas city's reported taxable value and total tax rate was extracted, and a taxable-value-weighted average rate was calculated for (1) all Texas cities statewide, (2) all cities located in Dallas County, and (3) the nine HD109 cities (Cedar Hill, Lancaster, DeSoto, Glenn Heights, Hutchins, Wilmer, Combine, Ferris, and Seagoville).
Weighting by taxable value (rather than a simple unweighted average) ensures the comparison is not skewed by small cities with disproportionately high or low rates.
Findings HD109 cities' weighted-average city property tax rate fell from $0.7426 per $100 of taxable value in 2017 to $0.6212 per $100 in 2025, a decline of about 16.3% (Texas Comptroller of Public Accounts, 2017, 2025) .
Over the same period, the Texas statewide average fell from $0.5665 to $0.5316 (-6.2%), and Dallas County's average fell from $0.6882 to $0.6348 (-7.8%).
Despite HD109's steeper rate cuts, HD109 cities were still taxed 16.8% above the Texas statewide average in 2025, though this is down sharply from a 31.1% gap in 2017.
Compared to the rest of Dallas County, HD109 cities went from a 7.9% rate premium in 2017 to essentially rate parity by 2025 (about 2.1% below the county average), showing that HD109's largest tax-rate reductions — led by Lancaster (-30.9%) and Glenn Heights (-36.4%) between 2017 and 2025 — outpaced the rest of the county.
Individual city rates within HD109 vary widely: Seagoville carried the highest 2025 rate at $0.7207 per $100 , while Combine carried the lowest at $0.3500 per $100 — a difference of more than double.
This means the "average" HD109 rate masks meaningfully different tax burdens depending on which HD109 city a constituent lives in.
Limitations These figures cover city-portion tax rates only — they do not include school district, county, community college, or special district rates, which for most HD109 homeowners make up the majority of a total property tax bill.
A full effective-rate or total-bill comparison would require combining city, county, school district, and special district rates for a representative property, which was outside the scope of this rate-only comparison.
2016 data could not be located from the Comptroller or web archives and is excluded rather than estimated.
DeSoto figures reflect the full city, though only the eastern half of DeSoto falls within HD109; Ferris straddles the Dallas/Ellis county line, and this dataset captures Ferris as reported under the Dallas Central Appraisal District.
Dollar-impact estimates referenced elsewhere (e.g., tax on a $300,000 home) are illustrative calculations applying the city rate only to a round-number taxable value, not actual average tax bills.

02 Total Property Tax Comparison — HD109 vs.
Dallas County vs.

Total Rate, 2025 (per $100) 1.873 Dallas County Avg, 2025 1.894 Texas Statewide Avg, 2025 +5.2% HD109 Above State Avg, 2025 Summary of Findings School districts are the single largest component of the total property tax bill in every geography examined: 1.014 of Texas's 1.894 core rate, 1.023 of Dallas County's 1.873, and 1.156 of HD109's 1.992 (Texas Comptroller of Public Accounts, 2025) .
HD109's estimated total property tax rate of 1.992 per $100 runs about 5.2% above the Texas statewide average of 1.894 and 6.4% above the Dallas County average of 1.873, driven mainly by higher ISD rates in DeSoto ISD, Lancaster ISD, and Cedar Hill ISD relative to the county and state averages.
Lancaster and Wilmer are the only HD109 cities with an identified special district (MUD) layer in this dataset — Lancaster MUD #1 (0.9494 per $100) and Wilmer MUD #1 (0.18 per $100) — which pushes their all-in totals meaningfully higher than the other seven HD109 cities.

For each taxing-unit type, a taxable-value-weighted average total tax rate was calculated statewide and for all units located in Dallas County.
The HD109 composite was built city-by-city: for each of the nine HD109 cities (Cedar Hill, Lancaster, DeSoto, Glenn Heights, Hutchins, Wilmer, Combine, Ferris, and Seagoville), the city's own weighted-average city rate was added to the total rate of its primary school district, the Dallas County rate, and any identified special district (MUD) rate whose name explicitly matches the city.
The nine city-level totals were then combined into a single HD109 composite using each city's own taxable value as the weight, so larger cities like DeSoto and Cedar Hill influence the composite more than smaller cities like Combine.
Findings The core total property tax rate (city + county + ISD) for the HD109 composite was 1.9925 per $100 of taxable value in 2025, compared to 1.8940 statewide and 1.8730 for all of Dallas County — meaning HD109's core rate ran about 5.2% above the state average and 6.4% above the Dallas County average .
When the two identified HD109 special districts (Lancaster MUD #1 and Wilmer MUD #1) are added, HD109's all-in composite rises to 2.2337 per $100 .
On an illustrative $300,000 home, that translates to roughly $6,701 per year in HD109 versus $6,108 in Dallas County overall and $6,031 statewide.
School districts are consistently the dominant component: ISD rates make up 58% of HD109's core total, 54% of the statewide core total, and 55% of the Dallas County core total.
Within HD109, DeSoto (2.126 per $100) and Lancaster (2.988 per $100 including its MUD) carry the two heaviest all-in rates, while Combine (1.735) and Wilmer (1.857) carry the lightest.
Limitations HD109 is not an official Comptroller reporting geography — it is a composite approximation built by the author from overlapping city, county, ISD, and special-district units.
Glenn Heights, Hutchins, Wilmer, Combine, Ferris, and Seagoville are each assigned to a single "primary" ISD (DeSoto ISD or Dallas ISD, based on the majority of the city's territory) even though small portions of some of these cities may fall in other districts (e.g., Ferris ISD also extends into Ellis County).
DeSoto figures reflect the full city; only the eastern half of DeSoto technically falls within HD109.
The special district search was limited to units whose name explicitly references an HD109 city; smaller or unnamed special districts (e.g., generic municipal utility districts, emergency services districts, or road districts) serving these cities may not be captured, so the "total incl. special" figures should be read as a floor rather than a ceiling on the special-district layer.
All dollar-impact figures are illustrative calculations applying the estimated rate to a round $300,000 taxable value, not actual average tax bills for a real property.

03 Sales Tax Stability & Growth Under a Fixed Rate How Texas sales & use tax revenue grew for 33+ years without a single rate increase, 1991–2024 6.25% State Rate, Unchanged Since 1991 +472% Revenue Growth, FY1991–FY2024 $47.24B FY2024 Collections (All Funds) +19.4% FY2022 Growth, Post-COVID Rebound +0.2% FY2020 Change — COVID Year Held Flat, Not Negative Summary of Findings The rate never moved, but revenue kept growing.
Texas set its state sales and use tax rate at 6.25% in 1990, effective fiscal 1991, and the Legislature has not raised it since.
Over the following 33 years, collections rose from $8.26 billion to $47.24 billion — a 472% increase driven entirely by a growing population, rising incomes, and expanding consumer spending, not by any tax increase.
Even COVID-19 did not produce a real decline.
Fiscal 2020, the year the pandemic hit hardest, saw collections essentially hold flat (up 0.2% to $34.04 billion) rather than collapse.
Growth resumed immediately in fiscal 2021 (+5.6%) and accelerated sharply in fiscal 2022 (+19.4%, to $42.99 billion) as the economy reopened and consumer spending surged — the strongest single-year gain of the entire 33-year fixed-rate period.
A fixed-rate consumption tax captures economic growth automatically.
Because the tax is a percentage of what people actually spend, a bigger economy and a bigger population mean bigger dollar collections at the exact same rate — no vote, no rate hike, and no reassessment required.
This is the core mechanical argument for why a broad sales tax can fund government more predictably, and with more built-in room to eventually lower the rate, than a property tax system tied to fluctuating home valuations.

177; BLS CPI-U annual averages. ↓ Download CSV Read the Full Report ▾ What This Shows Texas set its state sales and use tax rate at 6.25% in 1990, effective fiscal year 1991, and the rate has not changed in the 33 fiscal years since (Texas Comptroller of Public Accounts, 2025) .
This report tracks what happened to actual dollar collections over that same period, using the Comptroller's own year-by-year net collections data, to show what a fixed-rate consumption tax does as the underlying economy grows.
Methodology All revenue figures come directly from the Texas Comptroller of Public Accounts' Sources of Revenue: A History of State Taxes and Fees, 1972-2024 report (January 2025 edition), specifically its Sales and Use Tax net collections table, which reports "All Funds" collections for every fiscal year from 1972 through 2024 (Texas Comptroller of Public Accounts, 2025) .
Fiscal years run September 1 through August 31.
Inflation adjustments use the U.S.
Bureau of Labor Statistics' Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U), annual averages, rebasing every year's nominal dollar figure into constant fiscal 2024 dollars (U.S.
Bureau of Labor Statistics, 2026) .
The revenue growth index in the third tab simply divides each year's nominal revenue by the fiscal 1991 baseline and multiplies by 100, so a reader can see the pace of growth in relative terms independent of dollar scale.
Findings Nominal sales tax collections grew from $8.26 billion in fiscal 1991 to $47.24 billion in fiscal 2024, a 472% increase , without a single change to the 6.25% state rate (Texas Comptroller of Public Accounts, 2025) .
Even after removing the effect of inflation, real collections still grew from the equivalent of $19.01 billion to $47.24 billion in constant 2024 dollars — meaning a large share of the growth reflects genuine economic and population expansion in Texas, not just rising prices (Texas Comptroller of Public Accounts, 2025); (U.S.
Bureau of Labor Statistics, 2026) .
The COVID-19 pandemic produced only a brief pause, not a collapse.
Fiscal 2020 collections rose a modest 0.2% to $34.04 billion even as the pandemic shut down large parts of the economy for months (Texas Comptroller of Public Accounts, 2025) .
Growth resumed the following year (+5.6% in fiscal 2021) and then accelerated sharply to +19.4% in fiscal 2022, the single largest annual increase of the entire 33-year fixed-rate period, as reopening, stimulus-driven spending, and inflation combined to push collections to $42.99 billion (Texas Comptroller of Public Accounts, 2025) .
By fiscal 2024, collections reached $47.24 billion, roughly 5.7 times the fiscal 1991 level in nominal terms.
Two prior downturns are visible in the data and provide useful context: the dot-com/early-2000s slowdown produced a mild dip in fiscal 2002-2003, and the 2008-2009 financial crisis produced a two-year decline (fiscal 2009 down 2.7%, fiscal 2010 down a further 6.6%) before growth resumed in fiscal 2011 (Texas Comptroller of Public Accounts, 2025) .
Both recoveries confirm the same pattern seen after COVID: a broad consumption tax dips only when actual consumer spending genuinely contracts, and rebounds automatically — without any legislative action — once spending recovers.
Why This Matters for a Sales-Tax-Based System Because a sales tax is levied as a percentage of what people actually spend, a bigger economy and a bigger population mechanically produce bigger dollar collections at the exact same rate.
No vote, no reassessment, and no rate increase were needed to grow Texas sales tax revenue nearly six-fold since 1991 — population growth, income growth, and business formation did that automatically (Texas Comptroller of Public Accounts, 2025) .
This is the central mechanical argument for why a jurisdiction relying on a broad sales tax, rather than a property tax tied to home valuations, can experience rising revenue purely from organic economic growth, and — if a taxing entity ever collects more than it needs to fund its services — has room to lower its rate over time rather than needing to raise it.
Limitations The 6.25% state rate has been fixed since 1991, but the tax base (what specifically is taxed) has seen minor legislative adjustments over the same period — including a temporary fireworks tax that was later repealed, changes to the Texas Emissions Reduction Plan (TERP) surcharge on off-road diesel equipment, a 2019 requirement that online marketplace providers collect the tax, and targeted exemptions added in 2023 for items such as maternity and baby hygiene products (Texas Comptroller of Public Accounts, 2025) .
These base changes mean a small share of long-run revenue growth reflects adjustments to what is taxed, not solely economic and population growth, though the rate itself never changed.
Local sales taxes (city, county, transit, and special-district add-ons, capped at a combined 2 percentage points on top of the state rate) are excluded from this analysis, which covers only the state's 6.25% portion (Texas Comptroller of Public Accounts, 2025) .
References Texas Comptroller of Public Accounts.
(2025).
Sources of Revenue: A History of State Taxes and Fees, 1972-2024 . comptroller.texas.gov Official biennial report from the Texas Comptroller's office covering the full legislative and revenue history of every major state tax since 1972, including year-by-year net collections and every rate change for the state sales and use tax.
This is the primary source for every revenue and rate figure in this module.
U.S.
Bureau of Labor Statistics.
(2026).
Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U): U.S. city average, annual averages . bls.gov/cpi Federal government's official measure of consumer price inflation, used here to convert each fiscal year's nominal sales tax revenue into constant fiscal-2024 dollars so real (inflation-adjusted) growth can be distinguished from growth caused purely by rising prices.
04 Texas Median Household Income vs.
Median Home Value, 1970–2024 Both series converted to constant 1966 dollars using the BLS Consumer Price Index (CPI-U) to isolate real purchasing-power change from inflation 3.72× Home Value ÷ Income Ratio, 2024 1.41× Home Value ÷ Income Ratio, 1970 +17% Real Income Growth, 1984–2024 +48% Real Home Value Growth, 1984–2024 $302,999 TX Median Home Value, 2024 (Nominal) $81,490 TX Median HH Income, 2024 (Nominal) Summary of Findings Texas home values have grown almost three times faster than household income in real, inflation-adjusted terms since 1984.
Real median household income rose about 17% from 1984 to 2024, while real median home value rose about 48% over the same period (Federal Reserve Bank of St.
Louis, 2025; Federal Housing Finance Agency, 2026) .
The home-value-to-income ratio has nearly tripled since 1970.
A median Texas home cost about 1.4 times median household income in 1970; by 2024 it cost about 3.7 times median household income, with the sharpest jump occurring between 2020 and 2024.
Real household income was essentially flat for three decades.
Adjusted for inflation, Texas median household income in 2000 was barely above its 1970 level, and dipped below it during the 1980s oil-bust years, before modest real gains resumed after 2010.

Calculations by author. ↓ Download PNG Table Full Annual Series, 1984–2024 Nominal and constant-1966-dollar values, Texas median household income and median home value Year HH Income (Nominal) Home Value (Nominal) CPI-U HH Income (1966 $) Home Value (1966 $) Source: FRED (Census CPS ASEC); FHFA House Price Index (interpolated); BLS CPI-U. ↓ Download CSV Read the Full Report ▾ Overview This report compares the change in Texas median household income against the change in Texas median home value from 1969/1970 through 2024, with all dollar figures converted into constant 1966 dollars to isolate real purchasing-power change from inflation.
The earliest year for which reliable, state-specific Texas data could be located was 1969 (income, from the 1970 Census) and 1970 (home value, from the 1970 Census of Housing); a complete continuous series back to 1966 itself does not exist in Census records, so 1966 is used only as the price-deflation base year via the Bureau of Labor Statistics Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) (Bureau of Labor Statistics, 2025) .
In real, inflation-adjusted terms, Texas median household income grew modestly and unevenly — even declining during some periods — while Texas median home values grew far faster and more consistently, especially after 2010.
The result is that median home value went from roughly 1.4 times median household income in 1970 to roughly 3.7 times median household income in 2024, meaning the affordability gap between what a typical Texas household earns and what a typical Texas home costs has more than doubled in real terms over this period.
Data Sources & Methodology Income series.
Decennial Census benchmark values for Texas median household/family income were assembled directly from U.S.
Census Bureau decennial releases: $8,490 for 1969 (American Statistical Association Proceedings, 1972) , $16,469 for 1979 (United Press International, 1982) , $27,016 for 1989 and $39,927 for 1999 (Demographia, 2001) .
From 1984 forward, the annual Current Population Survey (CPS) Annual Social and Economic Supplement series for Texas — as compiled by the Federal Reserve Bank of St.
Louis (FRED) from Census Bureau data — provides year-by-year figures through 2024 (Federal Reserve Bank of St.
Louis, 2025) .
Home value series.
Decennial Census "Historical Census of Housing Tables" provide unadjusted (nominal) Texas median home values for 1970 ($12,000), 1980 ($39,100), 1990 ($59,600), and 2000 ($82,500) ("Median Home Values: Unadjusted," n.d.) .
The 2010 figure ($128,900) comes from American Community Survey estimates as compiled by the Texas Demographic Center (Texas Demographic Center, 2015) , and the 2020 figure ($187,200) and 2024 figure ($302,999) come from the Zillow Home Value Index for Texas as republished via FRED, cross-validated against Zillow's own current reporting (Zillow, 2026) .
Because decennial Census and ACS benchmarks for home value are only available roughly once per decade, annual-resolution movement between benchmark years was estimated using the Federal Housing Finance Agency's (FHFA) All-Transactions House Price Index for Texas, available annually from 1975 forward (Federal Housing Finance Agency, 2026) .
The FHFA index was scaled piecewise by the author so that it exactly matches the known Census/ACS dollar benchmarks at each decade point, with the index used only to approximate the shape of the path between those fixed points.
This is a calculation performed by the author, not a figure drawn directly from any single source, and is disclosed here per the requirement to identify inferential or calculated data distinctly from directly reported source data.
All nominal dollar figures were converted to constant 1966 dollars using annual average CPI-U index values from the Bureau of Labor Statistics (Bureau of Labor Statistics, 2025) , using the formula: real value = nominal value × (CPI-U in 1966 ÷ CPI-U in the observed year).
Findings: Decade Benchmarks Texas real median household income was essentially flat for the first 30 years of this period: it was actually lower in real terms in 2000 ($7,512 in 1966 dollars) than a household earning the 1970 median might expect after even modest real growth, and real income dipped notably in the 1980s before recovering.
By contrast, real median home value grew in every decade except the 1980s, when Texas experienced an oil-bust housing downturn that temporarily depressed home values even in nominal terms.
The home-value-to-income ratio rose from 1.41 in 1970 to 2.37 in 1980, eased slightly to 2.07 by 2000, then climbed steadily to 2.56 in 2010, 2.74 in 2020, and 3.72 by 2024 — the sharpest single jump of the entire series occurring in the four years between 2020 and 2024.
Findings: Annual Trend, 1984–2024 Using the annual FRED income series and the FHFA-interpolated home value series, real median household income in Texas grew from roughly $7,179 (1966 dollars) in 1984 to about $8,417 in 2024 — a real increase of only around 17 percent over 40 years.
Over the same period, real median home value in Texas rose from roughly $21,082 to $31,296 (1966 dollars) — a real increase of roughly 48 percent, with nearly all of that increase occurring after 2012 and especially after the COVID-19 pandemic-era housing boom of 2020–2022.
When both series are indexed to 1984 = 100, the real household income index stood at roughly 117 by 2024 (a 17 percent real gain), while the real home value index stood at roughly 148 (a 48 percent real gain) — visually confirming that Texas home prices have outpaced Texas household earning power in real terms, particularly after the 2008 financial crisis recovery and the 2020–2022 pandemic-era housing market surge.
Findings: The Affordability Ratio The home-value-to-income ratio — how many years of gross household income it would take to purchase the median home outright — fell from approximately 2.56 in 2010 to 2.74 in 2020, then jumped sharply to roughly 3.72 by 2024, an increase of nearly one full year of household income needed to purchase a median home in just four years.
This acceleration coincides with historically low mortgage rates and pandemic-driven housing demand of 2020–2022, combined with continued population growth into Texas that outpaced new housing supply, as reflected in the FHFA index's largest recorded single-year Texas appreciation rates occurring in 2021 and 2022 (Federal Housing Finance Agency, 2026) .
A ratio below roughly 3.0 is generally considered affordable by historical housing-finance conventions; ratios above 4.0 to 5.0 are typically associated with severe affordability strain, meaning Texas's 2024 ratio of 3.72 sits meaningfully above the traditional affordability threshold.
Limitations No single continuous, annually reported, state-specific Texas dataset exists covering median household income and median home value back to 1966; the decennial Census only measured state-level median income and home value once every ten years prior to the American Community Survey's launch in the 2000s.
The 1969, 1979, 1989, and 1999 income figures and the 1970, 1980, 1990, and 2000 home value figures are therefore official Census-year snapshots rather than a continuous annual series.
The annual home value figures for years between decennial Census/ACS benchmarks were calculated by the author by scaling the FHFA All-Transactions House Price Index for Texas to match the known dollar benchmarks at each decade point; these interpolated values should be understood as a reasonable estimate of the trend rather than an official reported statistic for any single year other than the anchor years themselves.
This is a deductive calculation using an official, methodologically sound index (FHFA) anchored to official Census/ACS dollar figures.

05 Federal Healthcare vs.
Military Spending Historical federal ledger, household costs, health outcomes, and 2026 monthly Treasury outlays $21.09T Federal health spending, 2000–2024 $15.00T National defense outlays, 2000–2024 1.41× Cumulative health ÷ defense $1.599T Selected health outlays, Jan.–Aug.
2026 43–95× Monthly health ÷ $3B Iran scenario Summary of Findings The federal government spent more on healthcare than on national defense over 2000–2024: $21.087 trillion in CMS federal sponsor spending versus $15.004 trillion in OMB national-defense outlays, a cumulative ratio of 1.41 to 1.
The series use different accounting periods—calendar years for CMS and federal fiscal years for OMB—so the comparison establishes scale rather than a reconciled accounting identity (Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services [CMS], 2024) ; (Office of Management and Budget [OMB], 2026) .
Selected federal healthcare-related net outlays totaled $1.599 trillion from January through August 2026.
Each observed monthly total was 43.3 to 94.7 times the $3 billion high-intensity Iran-conflict benchmark.
The benchmark is a scenario—not an assertion that exactly $3 billion was spent in every month (Congressional Budget Office [CBO], 2026) .
More federal healthcare spending did not translate into lower household medical prices across the period.
Employer premiums, worker contributions, deductibles, out-of-pocket spending, and major medical price indexes generally rose, while health outcomes moved in different directions.
Those patterns are descriptive and do not, by themselves, establish that federal spending caused the changes.

CBO estimated roughly $38 billion in cumulative Department of Defense costs through August 1, 2026.
For an additional month, CBO estimated $2 billion at May–June intensity and $3 billion at approximately July intensity.
The flat $3 billion column below applies that upper benchmark to each month solely to show scale.

Scope notes and exact-dollar values remain in the downloadable project CSV supplied with this module's source package. ↓ Download CSV Read the methodology, limitations, and references ▼ Definition of federal healthcare spending.
The 2000–2024 historical series uses CMS National Health Expenditure Accounts federal sponsor spending.
The 2026 monthly series is narrower: it sums 16 healthcare-related Treasury MTS Table 5 program lines selected to avoid obvious subtotal duplication.
These two series answer different questions and are not spliced into one time series.
Defense and war accounting.
OMB national-defense outlays are federal fiscal-year outlays.
OCO/GWOT figures are budget authority for designated war accounts and overlap defense; they are presented separately.
The 2026 Iran-conflict benchmark comes directly from CBO and is incremental DoD cost, not a complete whole-of-government cost.
Iran-cost uncertainty.
CBO relied on government databases and public reporting because DoD did not respond to its information requests.
CBO's approximately $38 billion estimate through August 1 excludes costs borne outside DoD, basic military operating costs already in the budget, future veterans' health and disability costs, unverifiable base-repair costs, and some future maintenance.
Its $2–$3 billion figure is prospective and intensity-dependent.
Interpretation.
Charts showing legislation dates, correlations, simultaneous movement, household costs, or outcomes are descriptive.
They do not establish that a law or federal spending caused a subsequent change.
Blank values remain missing and are never converted to zero or interpolated unless an official source itself supplies an estimate.

06 U.S.
Household Income: Viral Chart vs.
Census Data Inflation-adjusted median household income, 1967–2025, using the Census Bureau's official CPS ASEC series. $87,460 2025 real median income Record high Highest in series since 1967 +55.5% Real increase, 1967–2025 +2.6% Change from 2024 Summary of Findings Verdict: the “record low” income chart is false and reverses the official trend.
Census reports real median household income of $87,460 in 2025—the highest value in the series dating to 1967—not roughly $52,000 (U.S.
Census Bureau, 2026) .
The official inflation-adjusted series rises from $56,230 in 1967 to $87,460 in 2025, a 55.5% increase.
The path is uneven, with recession-era declines and methodology breaks, but it does not resemble the near-continuous collapse shown in the viral image.
This measure is pretax money income and excludes in-kind transfers.
“Record real median income” describes the national midpoint after inflation adjustment; it does not mean housing, food, medical care, or every household's budget became easy to afford.

House Office of the Historian and Party Government Since 1857 . ↓ Download PNG Read the Full Report ▾ Claim evaluated The image labels a falling curve “U.S. household income at record low” and attributes it to the Census Bureau.
The displayed endpoint is near $52,000 in 2025, while the beginning is near the upper-$80,000 range.
Methodology The check uses Census Table 2, “Households by Total Money Income, Race, and Hispanic Origin of Householder: 1967 to 2025.” The all-races median series is reported in constant 2025 dollars, using C-CPI-U for 2000–2025 and R-CPI-U-RS before 2000 (U.S.
Census Bureau, 2026) .
Where Census publishes parallel estimates for survey redesign or population-control transition years, this module uses the first, currently preferred row shown in the 2025 table and does not average the alternatives.
The downloadable full series preserves one observation per calendar year and includes Census's published margin of error.
Findings Real median household income was $56,230 in 1967, $73,560 in 2000, $85,320 in 2019, and $87,460 in 2025.
The 2025 estimate was 2.6% higher than the revised 2024 estimate of $85,210 and was the highest on record dating to 1967 (U.S.
Census Bureau, 2026) .
The viral curve appears directionally inverted: its approximate starting level resembles the current official value, while its approximate 2025 endpoint resembles the earliest official value.
That is an inference from visual comparison, not proof of how the image was produced.
The defensible conclusion is that the displayed line and “record low” label do not match Census data.
Presidential Timeline The Presidential Timeline tab overlays the same official Census real median household income series with a dashed line at the first annual observation in each presidency.
Presidential names and tenure dates come from the House Office of the Historian (U.S.
House of Representatives, Office of the Historian, n.d.-b) ; Democratic or Republican control of the White House comes from the same office’s party-government record (U.S.
House of Representatives, Office of the Historian, n.d.-a) ; and service years were cross-checked against the Library of Congress chronological list (Library of Congress, n.d.) .
Because Lyndon Johnson took office on November 22, 1963, before the income series begins, his marker is pinned to the first available year, 1967.
The markers show which president held office when each annual estimate was recorded.
They do not isolate the effect of any administration from economic cycles, legislation passed by Congress, or methodology changes in the survey.
Limitations CPS ASEC estimates are survey estimates subject to sampling and nonsampling error.
The historical series also includes documented changes to population controls, processing, questionnaire design, and interpolation methods.
Census tests year-to-year comparative statements at the 90% confidence level and flags changes that are not statistically significant (U.S.
Census Bureau, 2026) .
Median household income is not a complete affordability index.
It does not directly compare income growth with rent, home prices, groceries, insurance, or regional cost differences, and pretax money income excludes noncash benefits.

07 U.S.
Poverty Rate: Viral Chart vs.
Census Data Official poverty measure, 1959–2025, plus the separate supplemental poverty measure for context.
10.2% Official poverty rate, 2025 Series low Lowest annual value, 1959–2025 −12.2 pp Change from 1959 34.5M People below OPM threshold 13.1% Supplemental measure, 2025 Summary of Findings Verdict: the “record high” poverty chart is false and reverses the historical record.
The official rate fell from 22.4% in 1959 to 10.2% in 2025—a decline of 12.2 percentage points, or 54.5% relative to the 1959 rate (U.S.
Census Bureau, 2026) .
In 2025, 34.5 million people were below the official threshold.
The rate declined from 10.7% in 2024 to 10.2%, and inspection of the complete official series shows 2025 is the lowest published annual value from 1959 through 2025.
A different measure—the Supplemental Poverty Measure—was 13.1% in 2025 and was not statistically different from 13.0% in 2024.
Its series starts in 2009, so it cannot support a chart labeled “official poverty rate, 1959–2025.” Historical Trend Benchmark Table OPM vs.

House Office of the Historian and Party Government Since 1857 . ↓ Download PNG Read the Full Report ▾ Claim evaluated The image labels a steadily rising curve “U.S. poverty rate at record high,” marks it as the official poverty rate for all people from 1959 to 2025, and attributes it to the Census Bureau.
Its curve begins near 5% and ends above 20%.
Methodology The check uses the all-races, all-people series in Census Table 3, “Poverty Status of People by Age, Race, and Hispanic Origin Using the Official Poverty Measure: 1959 to 2025.” Census computes the official measure by comparing pretax money income with thresholds that vary by family size and composition (U.S.
Census Bureau, 2026) .
For years with parallel estimates caused by redesigns or updated population controls, this module retains the first, currently preferred row in the 2025 table.
Counts are published in thousands and converted to millions only for display.
Findings The official rate was #% in 1959, declined rapidly during the #s, and then fluctuated mainly between roughly #% and #% from the early #s through the late #s.
It reached #% in 2019, rose to #% in 2020, and then declined to #% in 2025 (U.S.
Census Bureau, 2026) .
The image's endpoints closely resemble the official endpoints placed in reverse order: official poverty was above 20% near the start of the series and near 10% at the end.
That resemblance supports a reasonable inference that the visual was inverted or fabricated, but the image alone cannot establish who altered it or how.
Why the SPM differs The SPM includes refundable tax credits and noncash benefits such as SNAP and housing assistance, then subtracts necessary expenses including taxes, work expenses, child care, medical out-of-pocket costs, and child support paid.
It also adjusts thresholds for housing tenure and geographic housing costs.
The official measure does not make those adjustments (U.S.
Census Bureau, 2026) .
The SPM was 13.1% in 2025, statistically unchanged from 13.0% in 2024.
Because the published SPM series begins in 2009, substituting it cannot explain a purported 1959–2025 official-rate chart.
Presidential Timeline The Presidential Timeline tab overlays the same official Census official poverty-rate series with a dashed line at the first annual observation in each presidency.
Presidential names and tenure dates come from the House Office of the Historian (U.S.
House of Representatives, Office of the Historian, n.d.-b) ; Democratic or Republican control of the White House comes from the same office’s party-government record (U.S.
House of Representatives, Office of the Historian, n.d.-a) ; and service years were cross-checked against the Library of Congress chronological list (Library of Congress, n.d.) .
Because Dwight Eisenhower took office on January 20, 1953, before the poverty series begins, his marker is pinned to the first available year, 1959.
The markers show which president held office when each annual estimate was recorded.
They do not isolate the effect of any administration from economic cycles, legislation passed by Congress, or methodology changes in the survey.
Limitations Both measures are estimates from CPS ASEC and are subject to sampling and nonsampling error.
A lower national poverty rate does not show that every geographic area or demographic group improved equally, and it does not mean families just above the threshold face no hardship.
The official measure has recognized scope limits: it excludes noncash benefits and refundable credits, does not subtract medical or work expenses, and uses national rather than geographically adjusted thresholds.
Those limits are reasons to examine both OPM and SPM—not reasons to reverse the official historical series.

28, 2026 (Trump Weeks) $4.192 Highest Annual Average, Nominal (2022) $5.391 Highest Annual Average, Aug.
2026 Dollars (2012) Summary of Findings Recent prices moved in both directions under the current administration.
In the 89 weeks from January 20, 2025 through September 28, 2026, the national regular gasoline price averaged $3.416 per gallon, fell as low as $2.779 (week of January 12, 2026), peaked at $4.500 (week of May 11, 2026), and stood at $4.465 on September 28, 2026 — 43.6% above the week of January 20, 2025.
The 208 Biden administration weeks shown, from January 25, 2021 through January 13, 2025, averaged $3.457 and ended 27.2% above where they began.
Inflation changes which years look most expensive.
In the dollars of the day, 2022 had the highest annual average ($4.192).
Adjusted to August 2026 dollars, 2012 was the most expensive year ($5.391), July 2008 was the most expensive month ($6.229), and 1998 was the cheapest year ($2.291).
Counting every week gives a fairer comparison than peaks.
Filling a 16-gallon tank every week and converting each fill-up to August 2026 dollars, one year of fill-ups cost $2,731 in Trump’s first term, $3,255 in Biden’s term, and $2,888 so far in Trump’s second term — $524 more per year under Biden than Trump’s first term, and $367 less per year so far in Trump’s second term than under Biden.
Timing is not causation.
The presidential and congressional markers show who held office when prices changed.
They do not separate the effect of any policy from global crude oil markets, refining, taxes, seasonal fuel blends, wars, disasters, or recessions.

Period statistics calculations by author. ↓ Download CSV Chart · Bar Weekly Fill-Up Experiment: Cost of One Year of Fill-Ups One 16-gallon tank every week (52 tanks a year), priced at each week’s U.S. average; inflation-adjusted to August 2026 dollars, with the nominal cost shown for reference Inflation-adjusted (Aug.
2026 $) Nominal (dollars of the day) Trump terms Biden term Biden’s term cost a weekly driver $524 more per year than Trump’s first term (+19.2%), in August 2026 dollars.
Trump’s second term so far is running $367 less per year than Biden’s term (-11.3%).
Per-year figures let full four-year terms and a term in progress be compared on the same scale.
Trump 2nd-term inflation-adjusted figures use 81 of 89 weeks; the dataset has no CPI-U value for October 2025 or September 2026, so those weeks are left out of real-dollar figures rather than estimated.
Source: U.S.

16-gallon weekly fill-up model and inflation adjustment calculated by author. ↓ Download CSV Read the Full Report ▾ What This Shows This report places official U.S. gasoline price data alongside the dates each president and each congressional majority held office.
It answers a narrow factual question — what did gasoline cost, and who held office at the time — and it does not attempt to measure how much any president or Congress caused prices to rise or fall.
Retail fuel prices also move with global crude oil markets, refinery capacity, taxes, seasonal fuel blends, wars, natural disasters, recessions, and demand shocks.
Data and Methodology Recent weekly series.
The January 2021 – September 2026 zoom uses the weekly U.S.
Regular All Formulations Retail Gasoline Price, reported in dollars per gallon including taxes, from the release of September 29, 2026, through the week of September 28, 2026 (U.S.
Energy Information Administration [EIA], 2026c) .
Weeks dated January 13, 2025 and earlier are grouped as Biden administration weeks; weeks dated January 20, 2025 and later are grouped as Trump administration weeks, matching the January 20, 2025 inauguration date (U.S.
House of Representatives, Office of the Historian, n.d.) .
Period means, medians, minimums, maximums, and first-to-last-week changes were calculated by the author from those 297 weekly observations.
Long-run monthly series.
Monthly gasoline prices are the BLS U.S. city average price for unleaded regular gasoline (series APU000074714), available from January 1978 through August 2026 (U.S.
Bureau of Labor Statistics [BLS], 2026a) .
Monthly on-highway diesel prices, available from January 1995, and domestic crude oil first-purchase prices, available from January 1974, come from the EIA Monthly Energy Review (EIA, 2026b) ; (EIA, 2026a) .
Inflation-adjusted values convert each month to August 2026 dollars with the formula: nominal price × (August 2026 CPI-U ÷ CPI-U in the observation month), using the BLS Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (BLS, 2026b) .
Missing months were left blank and were not interpolated.
Political periods.
Presidential term dates come from the House Office of the Historian (U.S.
House of Representatives, Office of the Historian, n.d.) , and House and Senate party control by Congress comes from the U.S.
Senate party-division record and the same House source (U.S.
Senate, n.d.) ; (U.S.
House of Representatives, Office of the Historian, n.d.) .
In the monthly term groupings, the month of an inauguration is assigned to the incoming term (for example, January 2025 is grouped with the 2025–2026 term).
Congressional-control groups pool every month with the same combination of House and Senate majorities, even when those months are decades apart.
Findings: January 2021 – September 2026 Across the 208 weeks from January 25, 2021 through January 13, 2025, the national regular gasoline price averaged $3.457 per gallon, ranging from $5.006 (week of June 13, 2022) down to $2.392 (week of January 25, 2021).
The final week of that period, $3.043, was 27.2% above the first full week of the term, January 25, 2021 ($2.392) (EIA, 2026c) .
Across the 89 weeks from January 20, 2025 through September 28, 2026, the price averaged $3.416 , with a low of $2.779 (week of January 12, 2026) and a high of $4.500 (week of May 11, 2026).
The September 28, 2026 price of $4.465 was 43.6% higher than the $3.109 recorded the week of January 20, 2025 (EIA, 2026c) .
Because the two periods cover different lengths of time and different seasons, their averages are descriptive comparisons, not measures of policy performance.
Findings: Long-Run History, 1978–2026 Measured in the dollars of the day, the highest annual average gasoline price in the series was $4.192 per gallon in 2022 , and the highest single month was June 2022 at $5.058 (BLS, 2026a) .
After adjusting for inflation to August 2026 dollars, the picture changes: the highest annual average was 2012 at $5.391 , the highest single month was July 2008 at $6.229, and the lowest annual average was 1998 at $2.291 (BLS, 2026a) ; (BLS, 2026b) .
By presidential term, the highest nominal monthly average belongs to the 2021–2025 Biden term ($3.563), followed by the 2025–2026 Trump term to date ($3.518) and the Obama terms ($2.974).
In August 2026 dollars, the ranking differs: the Obama terms averaged $4.349, the Carter months from 1978 averaged $4.219, and the Biden term averaged $4.038, while the Clinton terms had the lowest real average at $2.533 (BLS, 2026a) ; (BLS, 2026b) ; (U.S.
House of Representatives, Office of the Historian, n.d.) .
By decade, the 2000s showed the greatest relative volatility (coefficient of variation 0.327), while the 1990s were the most stable (0.075) (BLS, 2026a) .
Grouped by congressional control, months under divided control averaged $2.273 nominal and $4.022 in August 2026 dollars; Democratic unified control averaged $1.749 nominal and $3.555 real; and Republican unified control averaged $1.986 nominal and $3.124 real (BLS, 2026a) ; (BLS, 2026b) ; (U.S.
Senate, n.d.) .
These pooled groups mix different decades, presidents, and world events, so they describe timing only.
Findings: Weekly Fill-Up Experiment Comparisons based on price peaks, or on which administration “had the highest price for the longest,” rest on a small number of weeks.
This experiment instead counts every week.
It assumes one 16-gallon fill-up each week at that week’s national average price for regular gasoline (EIA, 2026c) , then converts each fill-up into August 2026 dollars using the CPI-U for the month of that week, with the formula: nominal fill-up × (August 2026 CPI-U ÷ CPI-U in the fill-up month) (BLS, 2026b) .
Each term begins with the first weekly price on or after inauguration day and ends with the last weekly price before the next inauguration, so Trump’s first term covers 209 weeks, Biden’s term 208 weeks, and Trump’s second term 89 weeks through Sept.
28, 2026.
Because the terms differ in length, results are compared per 52 weekly fill-ups.
In August 2026 dollars, one year of weekly fill-ups cost $2,731 during Trump’s first term, $3,255 during Biden’s term, and $2,888 so far in Trump’s second term.
The average inflation-adjusted price per gallon was $3.28, $3.91, and $3.47, respectively, and the most expensive single fill-up was $63.10 (May 28, 2018), $90.55 (June 13, 2022), and $71.97 (May 11, 2026) (EIA, 2026c) ; (BLS, 2026b) .
Over complete terms, a weekly driver spent $10,976 during Trump’s first term and $13,022 during Biden’s term in August 2026 dollars.
Trump 2nd-term inflation-adjusted figures use 81 of 89 weeks; the dataset has no CPI-U value for October 2025 or September 2026, so those weeks are left out of real-dollar figures rather than estimated.
All calculations are the author’s.
The model fixes the amount of gasoline bought each week, so differences reflect prices only, not changes in how much people drove or in vehicle fuel economy.
Like the rest of this report, it shows what drivers paid while each president was in office, not what caused prices to change.
Limitations The weekly EIA series and the monthly BLS series are both official gasoline price measures, but they are different survey products, so a weekly EIA value should not be compared directly with a monthly BLS value for the same period (EIA, 2026c) ; (BLS, 2026a) .
Monthly gasoline data begin in January 1978, so terms and decades before that date have no gasoline observations in this analysis; the 1970s decade covers only 1978–1979, the Carter term covers only 1978–1980, and the 2020s and the 2025–2026 term are incomplete.
Annual averages for 2026 are excluded because the year is not complete.
None of the statistics here control for crude oil prices, inflation expectations, global events, or other factors, and none should be read as evidence that a particular officeholder caused a price change.

It supplies every monthly gasoline value from January 1978 through August 2026 used in the long-run charts, term, decade, and congressional-control statistics.

It supplies the on-highway diesel prices from January 1995 shown in the nominal and inflation-adjusted charts and the annual data table.

Weekly U.S. regular all formulations retail gasoline prices (dollars per gallon) [Data set; release of September 29, 2026]. eia.gov/dnav/pet/hist/LeafHandler.ashx?n=PET&s=EMM_EPMR_PTE_NUS_DPG&f=W EIA's weekly national average retail price for regular gasoline, including taxes.
All 297 weekly observations from January 25, 2021 through September 28, 2026 in the Recent Zoom chart, comparison table, and weekly data table come from this series.

It provides the inauguration dates used for the presidential markers and term groupings.

Combined with House control, it defines the Democratic unified, divided, and Republican unified groupings used in the congressional-control chart and statistics.
09 Teacher Pay, Funding, Enrollment, and Texas District Grades How per-student funding, average teacher pay, and district size line up with the A–F grades of 1,187 Texas school systems in 2023–24, with a 10-year look back to 2014–15 0.07 Funding vs.
Score Correlation, 2023–24 0.05 Teacher Pay vs.
Score Correlation, 2023–24 0.01 District Size vs.
Score Correlation, 2023–24 −0.49 Student Poverty vs.
Score Correlation, 2023–24 +1.2 Percentile Points per 10% Real Funding Gain, Same District, 2018–2024 Summary of Findings Funding, teacher pay, and district size each had almost no relationship with a district’s 2024 grade on their own.
Among 1,016 traditional districts in 2023–24, the correlation with the overall score was 0.07 for operating revenue per student in attendance, 0.05 for average teacher base salary, and 0.01 for district size.
The 15 F-rated districts had the highest median funding of any grade ($19,604) (Texas Education Agency [TEA], n.d.-d) ; (TEA, n.d.-g) ; (TEA, n.d.-b) ; (TEA, n.d.-c) .
Student poverty lined up with grades far more than anything else.
The economically disadvantaged share correlated −0.49 with the overall score.
83% of the lowest-poverty fifth of districts earned an A or B, compared with 28% of the highest-poverty fifth, even though the highest-poverty districts received more per student (TEA, n.d.-d) ; (TEA, n.d.-c) ; (TEA, n.d.-g) ; (TEA, n.d.-b) .
Among districts serving similar students, more money and pay go with small gains.
Holding student need, size, district type, and region constant, 10% more funding per student went with about 0.34 points on the 100-point score and $1,000 more in average salary with about 0.16 points.
Within the same district over the decade, a 10% real funding increase went with about 1.2 percentile points higher standing and $1,000 in real pay with about 0.39 points (TEA, n.d.-d) ; (TEA, n.d.-g) ; (TEA, n.d.-b) ; (TEA, n.d.-c) ; (TEA, n.d.-a) ; (TEA, n.d.-h) .
The 10-year record shows these are long-standing patterns.
From 2014–15 to 2023–24, median real funding per enrolled student rose 11.6% while median real teacher pay fell 4.1% and enrollment grew 2.1%.
In every letter-grade year, poverty correlated between −0.50 to −0.38 with scores, while funding stayed between −0.04 to 0.09 (TEA, n.d.-a) ; (TEA, n.d.-d) ; (TEA, n.d.-g) ; (TEA, n.d.-h) ; (TEA, n.d.-c) ; (U.S.
Bureau of Labor Statistics [BLS], 2026) .
A district’s grade conceals wide campus differences, and none of this proves cause.
In 38% of traditional districts with five or more letter-graded campuses, at least one campus earned an A while another earned a D or F (TEA, n.d.-d) .
All results are associations, measured across changes in TEA’s grading rules.

Districts were matched by six-digit TEA district number across five TEA files, covering 1,246 school systems.
Every source row is kept in the master file.
Records that fall outside the analysis are flagged, not deleted.
The three-standard-deviation screen excludes, measure by measure, values more than three standard deviations from the statewide mean of the analysis population.
Interquartile-range screened and winsorized versions, along with every charter result, are in the companion file descriptives_by_rating.csv .

For each measure, traditional districts were sorted from lowest to highest and divided into five groups of equal size (quintiles).
Each row then reports the group’s median overall score, the share rated A or B and D or F, and the group’s medians on the other measures.
This shows how the measures move together: for example, low-funded districts tend to be large and to pay higher salaries.
Source: TEA 2024 accountability, FY2024 PEIMS actual finances, 2023–24 Summary of Finances, and 2023–24 TAPR.

0.234 0.043 to 0.424 0.016 1016 How this table was produced.
The outcome is the 2024 district overall score.
Funding is FY2024 operating revenue per 2023–24 refined ADA, entered as a natural logarithm.
Student need is entered as percentage-point shares.
District size is the natural log of refined ADA.
Teacher pay is average base salary in thousands of dollars.
District-weighted models count each district equally; ADA-weighted models give larger districts proportionally more weight.
Charter and all-system models are in the companion file model_coefficients.csv .
Source: Calculated from TEA 2024 accountability, FY2024 PEIMS actual finances, 2023–24 Summary of Finances, and 2023–24 TAPR.

Each district-year record joins TEA PEIMS revenue and fall enrollment for the fiscal year, the TAPR district profile for the same school year, and the district’s rating and score.
Records require positive enrollment and revenue and a reported average teacher salary.
Dollar amounts are converted to FY2024 dollars using the September–August average of CPI-U.
The full panel, with charter systems, is in the companion file district_panel_2015_2024.csv .

percentile rank -0.385 -0.432 to -0.337 0.000 1011 How this table was produced.
The cross-section models repeat the comparable-district analysis for each letter-grade year, using funding per enrolled student and fall enrollment.
The fixed-effects model pools all five letter-grade years and includes a separate constant for each district and each year, so it compares each district only with itself over time.
The change model relates each district’s change in percentile rank from 2018–19 to 2023–24 to its changes in real funding, real teacher pay, enrollment, and poverty share, controlling for its 2018–19 rank.
Source: Calculated from TEA statewide accountability summary, 2024 accountability files, TEA PEIMS actual financial data, TEA TAPR district profiles, 2023–24 district type, and BLS CPI-U.
(TEA, n.d.-a) ; (TEA, n.d.-d) ; (TEA, n.d.-g) ; (TEA, n.d.-h) ; (TEA, n.d.-c) ; (TEA, n.d.-e) ; (BLS, 2026) Note: Cross-section models predict the overall score.
Fixed-effects and change models predict percentile rank within year.
Multiply a natural-log coefficient by 0.0953 for a 10% change. ↓ Download CSV Table · Data Source Registry — All Data Elements Original Texas Education Agency and BLS files, with the date each was accessed Data Element Source Original File Accessed 2024 district and campus A–F ratings and scores Texas Education Agency, accountability data download TEA_2024_accountability_district_summary.csv; TEA_2024_accountability_campus_summary.csv 2026-10-10 Rating rules and score definitions Texas Education Agency, 2024 Accountability Manual TEA_2024_manual.pdf 2026-10-10 Student characteristics and teacher pay, 2023–24 Texas Education Agency, TAPR data download TEA_TAPR_2023-24_District_*_Original.csv 2026-10-10 District revenue and spending, FY2024 Texas Education Agency, Summarized PEIMS Actual Financial Data tea-peims-summarized-actual-2009-2025.xlsx 2026-10-10 Refined ADA and weighted ADA, 2023–24 Texas Education Agency, Summary of Finances (final) 2023-2024-sof-run-id-45525.xlsx 2026-10-11 Summary of Finances field definitions Texas Education Agency, SOF data dictionary fy-2025-summary-of-finance-data-dictionary.pdf 2026-10-11 District type classification, 2023–24 Texas Education Agency, District Type Data district-type2324-0.xlsx 2026-10-11 District and campus ratings and scores, 2011–2023 Texas Education Agency, 2023 enhanced statewide summary (after appeal) 2023-enhanced-statewide-summary-after-appeal-updated.xlsx 2026-10-11 District students, staff, and teacher pay, 2014–15 to 2022–23 Texas Education Agency, TAPR data downloads (district profile and reference) tapr_2015_D_PROF.csv … tapr_2023_D_REF.csv (18 files) 2026-10-11 District revenue and fall enrollment, FY2015–FY2023 Texas Education Agency, Summarized PEIMS Actual Financial Data tea-peims-summarized-actual-2009-2025.xlsx 2026-10-10 Consumer Price Index (CPI-U), monthly U.S.
