Note: The original graphic was used only as the claim being checked. ↓ Download CSV Read the Full Report ▾ By Will Campbell, Republican Candidate, Texas House of Representatives, House District 109 · October 17, 2026 Executive Summary This report checks a viral inflation graphic against Bureau of Labor Statistics data, then follows prices month by month from 2008 through September 2026.
The main findings: (U.S.
Bureau of Labor Statistics [BLS], 2026a) The graphic is accurate.
Average 12-month inflation was 1.88% under Trump’s first term, 4.98% under Biden, and 2.94% under Trump’s second term through August 2026 (2.99% with September).
(Section 1) The inflation rate is not the price level.
An average rate tells you how fast prices climbed, not how high they climbed.
A basket that cost $100 at 2016 prices cost $108.99 in January 2021, $132.36 in January 2025, and $140.39 in September 2026.
(Section 2) The steepest increases came during the Biden term.
Prices rose 21.4% from January 2021 to January 2025, and inflation peaked at 9.1% in June 2022.
Of the 26 months since 2008 with price jumps above 0.5%, 14 fell in the Biden term, as did 9 of the 15 largest.
Groceries rose 22.7% and gasoline 40.4%, and the pattern holds with gasoline taken out.
(Sections 3–4) Most of the price increase since 2017 occurred under Biden.
Prices rose 38.75% from January 2017 to September 2026.
Of that increase, 59.6% came between January 2021 and January 2025, about three times the share of either Trump term, and about 40% came in the two years Democrats controlled both chambers of Congress.
(Section 9) Both supply shocks and stimulus drove the surge.
Federal Reserve research finds pandemic income support added about 3 percentage points to inflation by the end of 2021.
The American Rescue Plan passed without a single Republican vote.
The Inflation Reduction Act came after the peak and, by CBO’s estimate, had a negligible effect on inflation.
(Jordà et al., 2022) (Congressional Budget Office [CBO], 2022) (Section 5) Prices have not come back down, and they rarely do.
Since the 2022 peak, overall prices dipped in only 3 months, all by tiny amounts.
Since 1950 the yearly price change has turned negative only three times, and the two larger episodes came with recessions.
(National Bureau of Economic Research [NBER], 2023) (Section 6) Trump’s second term added a smaller increase, much of it from the Iran war.
Inflation averaged 2.62% before the war began on February 28, 2026; gasoline then jumped 21.2% in March.
Leaving out energy, prices are up 4.7% since January 2025, compared with 20.6% over the Biden term.
(Congressional Research Service [CRS], 2026) (Section 7) Without the Biden-era increase, prices would be 11% to 18% lower today.
Keeping every actual change since January 2025, the $140.39 basket would cost $115.60 if prices had been frozen, $124.56 at the Trump I pace, or $125.13 at the Federal Reserve’s 2% target.
These are hypothetical scenarios, not estimates of any one policy’s effect.
(Section 8) Bottom Line The data show when prices rose, not who caused every increase.
They do show that most of today’s higher price level was set from 2021 to 2024, that the laws passed in those years did not bring it down, and that families are still paying it.
(Section 9) 1.
Checking the Graphic First The graphic circulating on social media, credited to MRC with “Source: bls.gov,” lists average inflation of 1.9% for Trump’s first term, 5.0% for Biden, and 2.9% for Trump’s second term.
To test it, we downloaded the Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) directly from the Bureau of Labor Statistics and averaged every monthly 12-month inflation reading in each term, counting each term from the first full month in office.
(U.S.
Bureau of Labor Statistics [BLS], 2026a) The result: Trump I averaged 1.88% , Biden averaged 4.98% , and Trump II averaged 2.94% using data available through August 2026, when the graphic was posted.
Rounded to one decimal place, all three match.
With September’s data, released October 14, Trump II now averages 2.99%, which rounds to 3.0%.
(BLS, 2026b) Verdict on the Graphic Accurate as published.
Two clarifications matter.
First, Trump’s second term is only 19 months old, so its average can still change.
Second, an average inflation rate tells you how fast prices were climbing, not how high they climbed.
That difference is the most important idea in this report, and it is explained in Section 2.
Averages can hide a lot, so the chart stack below also shows every individual month.
Trump I readings stayed in a narrow band between 0.12% and 2.95%.
Biden-term readings ranged from 1.68% to 9.06%, with 40-year highs in 2022.
Trump II readings have ranged from 2.31% to 4.25%.
2.
Inflation Rate vs.
Price Level: The Idea That Explains Everything Two Different Measurements The price level is what things cost today.
The inflation rate is how fast that cost is rising, usually measured against the same month a year earlier.
When inflation “comes down” from 9% to 3%, prices are still going up, just more slowly.
The higher prices stay, and the new increases stack on top of them.
Think of a staircase.
The inflation rate is the height of each new step.
The price level is how high you have climbed.
During 2021 and 2022 the steps were the tallest in 40 years, peaking at 9.1% in June 2022.
The steps got shorter in 2023 and 2024, but the staircase never went back down.
Every family that felt the jump at the grocery store or the gas pump is still standing on that higher step.
(U.S.
Bureau of Labor Statistics [BLS], 2026a) The second and third tabs below translate this into dollars.
A basket of everyday goods and services that cost $100 at 2016 prices cost $101.18 when Trump took office in January 2017 and $108.99 when Biden took office in January 2021.
It cost $132.36 when Biden left office in January 2025, and costs $140.39 as of September 2026.
Put the other way, $100 today buys what $71.23 bought in 2016.
3.
Where the Price Increases Actually Happened This is the most important chart in this report.
It shows the month-to-month change in prices for every month from January 2008 through September 2026, using the seasonally adjusted CPI, which removes normal seasonal swings so months can be compared fairly.
The bars are each month’s increase.
The dark line is the cumulative price level that results.
(U.S.
Bureau of Labor Statistics [BLS], 2026a) Three things stand out: The tallest bars cluster in 2021 and 2022.
Since 2008 there have been 26 months in which overall prices rose more than half a percent in a single month.
14 of them came during the Biden term , and the other 12 are spread across the other five terms, almost 15 years in all.
Of the 15 largest monthly increases in the entire period, 9 happened under Biden.
The price line bends sharply upward in 2021.
Before 2021 the line climbs at a steady, gentle slope.
From 2021 through 2022 it turns steeply upward, then resumes a gentler climb from a much higher starting point.
The line essentially never goes down.
Since the inflation peak in mid-2022 there have been only 3 months in which overall prices dipped at all: Jul 2022 (-0.01%), Jun 2024 (-0.04%), Jun 2026 (-0.42%).
None came close to reversing the surge.
The “without gasoline” view leaves gasoline out of the chart so the smaller monthly changes in overall prices and food at home are easy to see.
Gasoline swings 10% or 20% in a single month, which flattens everything else on the same scale.
The “with gasoline” view adds gasoline in its own panel.
The last two tabs total the change within each term and list the largest months.
4.
Zooming In: The Pandemic Era and the Last Two Years Zooming in makes the timing clearer.
With prices set to 100 in December 2019, overall prices were only about 1% higher at the end of 2020.
From there it rose almost continuously, and the steepest part of the climb came from the spring of 2021 through the summer of 2022.
Food at home rose even faster than overall prices.
By September 2026 overall prices were about 30% above their December 2019 level and food at home about 32% above.
(U.S.
Bureau of Labor Statistics [BLS], 2026a) Gasoline tells a different story.
It is the most volatile thing households buy.
It collapsed in spring 2020, soared to about $5.06 a gallon in June 2022, the highest in the BLS series, and drifted down to around $3 by early 2026, before the Iran war pushed it back up (see Section 7).
Showing it in its own view keeps its swings from hiding the steady climb in everything else.
(BLS, 2026c) The 2024–2026 view shows what has happened since.
Overall prices kept rising at a slower, steadier pace through 2024 and 2025.
In spring 2026 gasoline jumped and overall prices rose faster for a few months.
Food at home has been nearly flat since spring 2026.
5.
What Drove the 2021–2022 Surge No single cause produced the surge, and honest analysis has to say so.
The research most often cited, including work by economists at the Federal Reserve, points to several forces acting together: Government spending and relief checks.
Researchers at the Federal Reserve Bank of San Francisco estimated that U.S. pandemic income-support measures added about 3 percentage points to U.S. inflation by the end of 2021.
That is a major reason U.S. inflation ran higher than in other wealthy countries.
(Jordà et al., 2022) The largest of these measures, the $1.9 trillion American Rescue Plan with its $1,400-per-person checks, was signed in March 2021.
It passed the House 220–211 without a single Republican vote and the Senate 50–49.
(Congress.gov, 2021) (Office of the Clerk, U.S.
House of Representatives, 2021) Supply shocks.
Shipping bottlenecks, factory shutdowns, and the shift in spending from services to goods pushed prices up directly.
A San Francisco Fed analysis attributed roughly half of the run-up to supply factors and about one-third to demand.
(Shapiro, 2022) Energy and the war in Ukraine.
Russia’s February 2022 invasion sent energy and food commodity prices soaring.
Energy prices were up 41.6% from a year earlier in June 2022.
(U.S.
Bureau of Labor Statistics [BLS], 2026a) A tight labor market.
Former Fed Chair Ben Bernanke and Olivier Blanchard found that most of the 2021–2022 rise came from price shocks.
They also found that fiscal policy added to it by boosting demand for goods already in short supply, and that tight labor markets became a bigger factor as the other shocks faded.
(Bernanke & Blanchard, 2023) What the Data Can and Cannot Show The charts show when prices rose.
Timing alone cannot assign an exact share of the increase to any one president, law, or event.
Some relief spending began under President Trump in 2020: the CARES Act’s $1,200 checks and December 2020’s $600 checks.
The Federal Reserve, not the president, sets interest rates.
What the record does show is that the largest stimulus, passed in early 2021, came right before the steepest climb in prices, and that research credits that spending with a meaningful part of the increase.
One more fact matters for anyone weighing the record.
The 2022 law branded the “Inflation Reduction Act” was signed in August 2022, two months after inflation had already peaked.
The Congressional Budget Office concluded it would have a “negligible effect on inflation” in 2022 and an effect of no more than 0.1 percentage point either way in 2023.
(Congressional Budget Office [CBO], 2022) Inflation did come down after mid-2022 as supply chains healed, energy prices fell back, and the Federal Reserve raised interest rates.
But nothing passed in Washington brought prices back down to where they had been.
6.
Why Prices Don’t Come Back Down Many people expect that once inflation “is beaten,” prices will return to where they were.
History says otherwise.
We checked every month of CPI data back to 1913 against the official recession dates kept by the National Bureau of Economic Research.
(U.S.
Bureau of Labor Statistics [BLS], 2026a) (National Bureau of Economic Research [NBER], 2023) What More Than a Century of Data Shows Since 1950, the 12-month change in consumer prices has turned negative only three times .
In 1954–55 it happened just after a recession (low of −0.7%).
In 2009 it happened during the Great Recession (low of −2.1%).
In 2015 it was a brief dip to −0.2% caused by an oil-price collapse.
Average prices for a full year have fallen only three times since 1940: in 1949, 1955, and 2009, each tied to a recession.
The deep price declines of the 1920s and 1930s came with depressions.
There is a reason.
Wages, rents, contracts, and business costs adjust upward over time and rarely reverse.
Falling prices across the whole economy usually happen only when demand collapses: when people lose jobs, businesses close, and spending dries up.
That is a recession, and no family wants that cure.
Since the 2022 peak, the economy has avoided recession, and as Section 3 showed, overall prices have dipped in only 3 months, all of them tiny.
“The price increases of 2021 and 2022 were not a temporary spike that will reverse.
Without a recession, a higher price level is the new floor, and every later increase is added on top of it.” — Finding from BLS CPI data, 1913–2026 7.
Trump’s Second Term and the Iran War Energy Shock From January 2025 through September 2026, overall prices rose 6.1%, against 21.4% during the four Biden years.
For the first year of the term, from February 2025 through February 2026, 12-month inflation averaged 2.62% .
Gasoline was cheaper in February 2026 (about $3.06 a gallon) than in January 2025 (about $3.21).
(U.S.
Bureau of Labor Statistics [BLS], 2026a) (BLS, 2026c) Then came the war.
The United States and Israel launched military operations against Iran on February 28, 2026, (Congressional Research Service [CRS], 2026) and oil shipments through the Strait of Hormuz fell sharply.
(U.S.
Energy Information Administration [EIA], 2026a) Gasoline prices jumped 21.2% in March alone, and the average gallon reached about $4.65 in May 2026.
The United States and Iran signed an agreement on June 18, 2026, to end the conflict, but energy prices have stayed elevated.
(EIA, 2026b) By September 2026, energy prices were up 20.4% from a year earlier and overall inflation was 3.7%.
(BLS, 2026b) Taking Energy Out of the Picture BLS publishes an index of all items less energy , which removes gasoline and household energy.
On that measure, prices rose 20.6% from January 2021 to January 2025 and 4.7% from January 2025 to September 2026.
In September 2026 that measure was up 2.6% over the year.
Most of the recent jump in headline inflation is energy, and energy is tied to the Iran war.
Prices outside energy are still rising faster than the Fed’s 2% goal, and Federal Reserve economists estimate the 2025 tariffs added about 0.8% to core consumer prices through February 2026.
That increase has been far smaller than the 2021–2024 run-up.
(U.S.
Bureau of Labor Statistics [BLS], 2026a) (Minton et al., 2026) 8.
What If the Biden-Era Increase Had Not Happened?
To measure the size of what happened from 2021 through 2024, we asked a simple question: what would prices be today if those four years had gone differently?
The method is straightforward.
We start from the actual price level in January 2021.
We replace the actual change from January 2021 to January 2025 with an assumed rate.
Then we apply the actual price change that has happened since January 2025, including the Iran war energy shock, so the Trump II period is left exactly as it happened.
(U.S.
Bureau of Labor Statistics [BLS], 2026a) Prices frozen.
No price change at all for four years.
This is a mathematical outer limit, not a realistic outcome.
Trump I pace.
Prices keep rising at Trump’s first-term average of #% a year.
Fed target.
Prices rise at the Federal Reserve’s #% goal.
The basket that actually costs $140.39 today (from $100 at 2016 prices) would cost $115.60 under the frozen-prices scenario, $124.56 at the Trump I pace, and $125.13 at the Fed’s target.
Even in the two realistic scenarios, prices today would be about 11% lower than they are.
Translated to the pump, regular gasoline at about $4.48 today would be about $3.21 at the Trump I pace.
Ground beef at $6.79 a pound would be about $5.42.
How to Read These Scenarios This is a “what if,” not a measurement of blame.
Replacing those four years also removes the pandemic reopening, global supply shocks, and Russia’s invasion of Ukraine, which no president controlled.
It shows how much of today’s price level was set during those four years: roughly 74% of the total price increase since January 2021.
9.
The Bottom Line The graphic is accurate: inflation averaged about 1.9% under Trump’s first term, 5.0% under Biden, and about 2.9% so far in Trump’s second term.
The fuller record says even more: Most of the price increase happened during the Biden term.
Prices rose 21.4% from January 2021 to January 2025, about 74% of the total increase since Biden took office.
Measured from the start of Trump’s first term, those four years account for 59.6% of the entire increase through September 2026.
Fourteen of the 26 months since 2008 with price jumps above half a percent fell in his term.
Those prices stayed.
Outside of a recession, prices almost never fall.
The higher price level set in 2021–2022 is the floor that every family still pays today.
The policies passed in those years did not bring prices down.
Federal Reserve research links the 2021 stimulus to part of the surge.
The Inflation Reduction Act came after the peak and, by CBO’s own estimate, had a negligible effect on inflation.
Trump’s second term added a much smaller increase.
Prices rose 6.1% through September 2026, and most of the 2026 rise traces to the energy shock from the Iran war.
How Much of the Increase Since 2017 Came During the Biden Term?
A broader way to ask the question: of all the price growth since Trump first took office in January 2017, how much happened during the Biden term?
Prices rose 38.75% from January 2017 to September 2026.
Of that increase, 59.6% occurred between January 2021 and January 2025, about three times the share of either Trump term (19.9% for Trump I and 20.5% so far for Trump II).
(U.S.
Bureau of Labor Statistics [BLS], 2026a) In dollars: a basket that cost $100 in January 2017 costs $138.75 today.
Of that $38.75 increase, $23.10 was added during the Biden term , compared with $7.72 under Trump I and $7.93 so far under Trump II.
The first two years were the steepest.
Democrats controlled both the House and the Senate from January 2021 to January 2023, the Congress that passed the American Rescue Plan.
Prices rose 14.4% in those two years alone.
That is about 40% of the entire increase since 2017 , and two-thirds of the Biden-term increase.
Reading These Shares Fairly These shares measure when prices rose, not who caused each increase.
As Section 5 showed, supply shocks, the war in Ukraine, and 2020 relief spending signed by President Trump also fed the surge.
But for anyone weighing responsibility for today’s prices, the timing is clear: most of the increase since 2017 came during the Biden term, the largest part of it while Democrats controlled Congress, and the laws passed in those years did not bring prices back down.
A Fair Comparison It is fair to ask any president what he is doing about prices.
But blaming today’s price level on the current administration while leaving out the four years in which most of it was set is not a fair reading of the data.
The numbers do not let anyone pin an exact share of the surge on one person or one law; supply shocks and a war in Europe played large roles.
They do show that the steepest increases came during the Biden term, that the laws passed in that period did not lower prices, and that families are still living with the result.
Every number in this article can be checked.
The data tables below hold the monthly figures, and each chart can be downloaded.
The original BLS series are listed in the references.

Checking the ITEP graphic that says every income group but the richest 1% is paying more in 2026: what it adds together, taxes alone against prior law, the health-credit expiration, the price gap against CBO’s forecast, and the same test for the Biden term −$1,160 OBBBA Tax Change, Middle 20%, vs.
Prior Law (2026) 6 of 7 Income Groups With a Tax Cut vs.
Prior Law 93% Of the Graphic’s Added Cost That Is Prices ($2,260 of $2,420) 54% Of the Above-Forecast Price Gap Built After the Iran War Began 8× Larger Price Gap Under Biden (11.2% vs.
1.4% Above Forecast) Summary of Findings The graphic adds three different things together.
ITEP’s totals combine new OBBBA tax provisions, the expiration of the enhanced health-insurance credit, and prices that rose faster than CBO forecast.
For the middle 20%, the +$1,700 is $1,850 in higher prices, $230 from the credit, and a $380 tax cut (Institute on Taxation and Economic Policy [ITEP], 2026a) .
On taxes alone, the OBBBA is a tax cut for six of seven groups.
Against the law that would otherwise have applied in 2026, when the 2017 cuts expired, the middle 20% pays $1,160 less and the fourth 20% $2,570 less.
The poorest 20% see +$20.
The graphic leaves out those extensions (Institute on Taxation and Economic Policy [ITEP], 2026a) .
The health-credit increase was scheduled by Democrats.
The American Rescue Plan created the enhanced credit, and the Inflation Reduction Act extended it only through 2025.
Both passed the House with no Republican votes; the OBBBA did not change the expiration date (Congressional Research Service [CRS], 2026a) (Office of the Clerk, U.S.
House of Representatives, 2022) .
Most of the “paying more” is prices measured against a forecast.
Prices were 1.4% above CBO’s January 2025 forecast by Q3 2026, and about 54% of that gap built up after the Iran war began on February 28, 2026 (CBO, 2025a) (U.S.
Bureau of Labor Statistics [BLS], 2026a) .
The Biden-era price increase outweighs the tax cut.
Prices ended the Biden term 11.2% above CBO’s February 2021 forecast, about 8 times the Trump II gap.
Applied to actual household spending, that costs a middle-income household about $6,440 in 2026, 5.6 times its $1,160 tax cut; without it, that household would keep about $7,600 more a year (U.S.
Bureau of Labor Statistics [BLS], 2025) (Congressional Budget Office [CBO], 2021) .

Data Table What the Graphic Says vs.
What the Data Show ITEP graphic, October 2026 What the graphic says or implies What the data show “Every income group is paying more as a result of key Trump tax … policies” Counting taxes alone against the law that would otherwise have applied, the OBBBA cuts taxes for six of seven groups; the middle 20% saves $1,160.
The poorest 20% see +$20 (ITEP Fig.
6).
The tax piece is “new tax policies enacted as part of the OBBBA” The graphic leaves out the OBBBA’s largest provision for working families: stopping the 2017 tax cuts from expiring.
That alone saves the middle 20% $780 and the fourth 20% $1,500.
The health-credit loss is a Trump policy The enhanced credit was written to expire on January 1, 2026, by the Inflation Reduction Act, which passed without a single Republican vote.
The OBBBA did not change that date (CRS).
Households are “paying more” Mostly prices: $2,260 of the $2,420 average added cost (93%).
The price figure is measured against a CBO forecast, not against last year’s prices.
Higher prices are from Trump policies About 54% of the above-forecast price gap built up after the Iran war began on February 28, 2026, much of it gasoline and energy.
Tariffs did add some (about 0.8% to core PCE prices by Feb 2026, per a Federal Reserve note).
This is unusual By the same test, prices ended the Biden term 11.2% above CBO’s forecast, versus 1.4% so far under Trump II, about 8 times as large.

Source: See References. ↓ Download CSV Read the Full Report ▾ By Will Campbell, Republican Candidate, Texas House of Representatives, House District 109 · October 18, 2026 Executive Summary A graphic from the Institute on Taxation and Economic Policy (ITEP) says every income group except the richest 1% is “paying more” in 2026 because of President Trump’s tax and economic policies.
We downloaded ITEP’s own data, CBO’s forecasts, and BLS price data to see what is inside those numbers.
(Institute on Taxation and Economic Policy [ITEP], 2026a) The graphic adds three different things together.
Its totals combine (a) new tax provisions in the One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA), (b) the expiration of an enhanced health-insurance tax credit, and (c) prices that rose faster than a CBO forecast.
ITEP’s arithmetic is correct; the label is the problem.
(Section 1) On taxes alone, the OBBBA is a tax cut for six of seven income groups.
Measured against the law that would otherwise have applied in 2026, when the 2017 tax cuts were set to expire, the middle 20% pays $1,160 less and the fourth 20% pays $2,570 less, by ITEP’s own figures.
The graphic leaves those extensions out.
(Section 2) The health-credit increase was scheduled by Democrats.
The enhanced credit was created by the American Rescue Plan and extended by the Inflation Reduction Act only through 2025.
Both passed without a single Republican vote.
The OBBBA did not change the expiration date.
(Section 3) Most of the “paying more” is prices, measured against a forecast.
Prices make up 93% of the average added cost.
As of the third quarter of 2026, prices were 1.4% above what CBO forecast in January 2025, and about 54% of that gap built up after the Iran war began.
(Section 4) The same test is about 8 times worse for the Biden term, and it outweighs the tax cut.
Prices ended the Biden term 11.2% above the forecast CBO made in February 2021.
Applied to actual household spending, that gap costs a middle-income household about $6,440 in 2026, 5.6 times its $1,160 OBBBA tax cut.
Without the Biden-era increase, that household would keep about $7,600 more a year.
(Section 5) What is fair to say.
The richest #% do receive the largest tax cuts in dollars, and tariffs have raised some prices.
But “every income group is paying more because of Trump’s tax policies” is not what ITEP’s own tax numbers show.
(Section 6) Bottom Line The OBBBA lowers federal taxes for most working families in 2026.
The price increases families feel are real, but most of today’s price level was set from 2021 to 2024, and that Biden-era increase costs the typical family several times what the tax cut saves.
(Section 7) 1.
What the Graphic Adds Together A widely shared Facebook post features a graphic from the Institute on Taxation and Economic Policy (ITEP).
It shows one bar per income group: +$750 for the poorest 20%, +$1,700 for the middle 20%, and −$12,560 for the richest 1%.
Its own footnote explains that each bar adds together three separate items: new tax policies in the OBBBA, the expiration of the Enhanced Premium Tax Credit, and “price increases occurring during Trump’s second term beyond what was initially projected.” (Institute on Taxation and Economic Policy [ITEP], 2026a) ITEP’s full report breaks each bar into those three pieces, and we downloaded the underlying data file.
The pieces add up exactly to the graphic’s totals.
For the middle 20%, the +$1,700 is $1,850 in higher prices , $230 from the health credit expiring, and a $380 tax cut from new OBBBA policies.
(ITEP, 2026b) So the graphic is not mainly about taxes.
Across all households, prices account for $2,260 of the $2,420 in added costs, or 93%.
For every group from the middle 20% up, the OBBBA’s new tax policies are a cut, not an increase.
2.
Taxes Alone: The OBBBA Cut Taxes for Most Households The 2017 tax cuts (lower rates, a larger standard deduction, and a $2,000 child credit) were written to expire at the end of 2025.
Without the OBBBA, nearly every household would have faced a tax increase on January 1, 2026.
The OBBBA made those provisions permanent.
(Institute on Taxation and Economic Policy [ITEP], 2026a) ITEP’s graphic counts only the OBBBA’s new tax policies and treats the extensions as if they had already been law.
ITEP’s own Figure 6 shows the other comparison, against the law that would otherwise have applied.
On that basis, the OBBBA cuts taxes for six of seven income groups : Middle 20%: $1,160 less in federal taxes in 2026 Second 20%: $370 less; fourth 20%: $2,570 less Poorest 20%: +$20, essentially no change, because many in this group owe no income tax Even after adding the health-credit expiration, taxes are lower for the middle 20% and every group above it.
The richest 1% receive the largest cut in dollars, which is a fair point for critics.
But the claim that “every income group is paying more” does not hold for taxes.
The Provisions Families Hear About ITEP groups the tax breaks on tips, overtime, car-loan interest, and the extra senior deduction as “politically prominent proposals.” They save the middle 20% about $310 and the fourth 20% about $630 in 2026, on top of the rate and child-credit extensions.
(Institute on Taxation and Economic Policy [ITEP], 2026a) 3.
The Health Credit Was Written to Expire The second piece of the graphic is the end of the Enhanced Premium Tax Credit, a larger subsidy for health plans bought through the Affordable Care Act marketplace.
It is presented as a Trump policy.
The legislative record shows otherwise.
(Congressional Research Service [CRS], 2026a) The American Rescue Plan (March 2021) created the enhanced credit for 2021 and 2022 only.
It passed the House 220–211 with no Republican votes.
(Office of the Clerk, U.S.
House of Representatives, 2021) The Inflation Reduction Act (August 2022) extended it through 2025 and set the sunset date of January 1, 2026.
It passed the House 220–207 with no Republican votes.
(Congress.gov, 2022) (Office of the Clerk, U.S.
House of Representatives, 2022) The OBBBA changed other credit rules (eligibility checks and repayment) but, according to the Congressional Research Service, “did not affect expiration of the enhanced PTC.” (Congressional Research Service [CRS], 2026a) The basic premium credit continues in 2026; only the temporary enhancement ended.
(Internal Revenue Service [IRS], n.d.) Critics argue Congress should have extended it, and that is a fair policy debate.
But the increase follows the date Democrats wrote into law, not a change made by the OBBBA.
In Texas, where marketplace enrollment is high, ITEP estimates this piece at about $620 for the second 20%.
(ITEP, 2026b) 4.
The Price Piece Is Measured Against a Forecast The largest part of every bar is prices.
ITEP does not measure how much prices rose.
It measures the gap between actual inflation and what the Congressional Budget Office forecast in January 2025, then attributes that whole gap to Trump’s policies, naming tariffs, the Iran war, and immigration.
(Institute on Taxation and Economic Policy [ITEP], 2026a) We rebuilt that gap from CBO’s published forecast and BLS data.
From the fourth quarter of 2024 to the third quarter of 2026, CBO forecast prices would rise 4.2%.
They actually rose 5.6%, leaving prices 1.4% above the forecast .
(CBO, 2025a) (U.S.
Bureau of Labor Statistics [BLS], 2026a) The timing matters.
By the first quarter of 2026, after a year of tariffs, prices were only 0.64% above forecast.
The gap more than doubled after the United States and Israel began military operations against Iran on February 28, 2026, and gasoline jumped 21% in a single month.
(CRS, 2026b) About 54% of the gap built up after that.
Had all prices grown like non-energy prices after February 2026, the price level in the third quarter would have been about 0.9% lower, which is roughly 62% of the gap.
What This Does and Does Not Show Tariffs did raise some prices.
A Federal Reserve analysis estimates they added about 0.8% to core PCE prices through February 2026 (a different price index than the CPI).
(Minton et al., 2026) But a gap against a forecast is not the same as a policy effect.
Forecasts miss for many reasons, and a war-driven oil shock is the kind of event no forecast includes.
5.
The Same Test Applied to the Biden Term If prices running ahead of CBO’s forecast counts as a cost of the current administration’s policies, the same yardstick should apply to the last one.
In February 2021, CBO forecast that consumer prices would rise 1.9% in 2021, 2.2% in 2022, 2.3% in 2023, and 2.4% in 2024.
(Congressional Budget Office [CBO], 2021) Actual inflation was 6.8%, 7.1%, 3.2%, and 2.7%.
Over the four years, CBO expected prices to rise 9.1%; they rose 21.3%.
Prices ended the Biden term 11.2% above forecast , about 8 times the 1.4% gap so far under Trump II.
Even per year, the Biden-term gap (2.7% a year) was about 3 times as fast.
(U.S.
Bureau of Labor Statistics [BLS], 2026a) What the Biden-Era Increase Costs Families Compared With the Tax Cut Because prices rarely fall, that higher price level is still built into what families pay in 2026.
To put a dollar figure on it, we applied both forecast gaps to what households actually spend, using the Bureau of Labor Statistics Consumer Expenditure Survey for 2024, brought to 2026 prices.
(U.S.
Bureau of Labor Statistics [BLS], 2025) A middle-income household spends about $64,240 a year on goods and services.
Because prices are 11.2% above CBO’s 2021 forecast path, about $6,440 of that is the Biden-era increase above forecast .
Its OBBBA tax cut against prior law is $1,160 , so the Biden-era price increase costs it 5.6 times what the tax cut saves.
For the fourth 20%, it is $8,290 against a $2,570 tax cut.
Had the Biden-era increase not happened, the middle 20% would keep about $7,600 more a year : its $1,160 tax cut plus the $6,440 it would not be spending on the same purchases.
Measured the same way, the Trump II gap so far costs that household about $790.
That is less than ITEP’s $1,850, which uses ITEP’s own consumption model.
How These Figures Are Calculated 2026 spending = 2024 Consumer Expenditure Survey average annual expenditures minus personal insurance and pensions (payroll and retirement contributions, not consumer prices), raised by the CPI-U from its 2024 average to its January–September 2026 average.
The Biden-era cost is the share of that spending due to prices being 11.15% above CBO’s February 2021 path.
CE quintiles are by household income and do not match ITEP’s income ranges exactly, and the Top 20% tax cut is ITEP’s weighted average of its top three groups.
Like ITEP’s figure, these measure a forecast miss, not the effect of any one policy.
6.
Correcting the Graphic ITEP is a left-leaning research group, and its arithmetic checks out: the three pieces add to the totals in the graphic.
The problem is the headline.
It says families are “paying more as a result of key Trump tax and economic policies,” but most of the added cost is prices measured against a forecast, and the tax piece leaves out the extensions that prevented a tax increase for nearly everyone.
What Critics Get Right The richest 1% receive the largest tax cuts, about $28,550 a year in new provisions and $70,240 against prior law.
And CBO’s analysis of the full law, which also counts spending changes such as Medicaid and SNAP, found the lowest-income tenth of households would lose resources on average over 2026–2034.
That is a different question from the 2026 tax and price claim in the graphic, but it is part of a complete picture.
(CBO, 2025b) “On taxes alone, measured against the law that would otherwise have applied, the OBBBA cuts 2026 federal taxes for six of seven income groups, including $1,160 for the middle 20%.” — Finding from ITEP’s own Figure 6, October 2026 7.
The Bottom Line Taxes: the OBBBA lowered 2026 federal taxes for most working families by keeping the 2017 tax cuts from expiring, and added new breaks for tips, overtime, car loans, and seniors.
Health credit: the enhanced credit ended on the date written into the Inflation Reduction Act, which passed with only Democratic votes.
Prices: prices are 1.4% above CBO’s January 2025 forecast, more than half of that from the 2026 energy shock.
By the same test, the Biden term left prices 11.2% above forecast.
That higher price level has not come down, and it costs a middle-income family about $6,440 in 2026, 5.6 times its tax cut.
(See our companion report, Where Prices Went Up — and Why They Never Came Back Down .) A Fair Comparison Families are right that things cost more.
Most of that is the price level set from 2021 to 2024, plus the 2026 energy shock.
Calling it the result of a tax law that cut most families’ taxes mixes up three different things.
Keeping taxes low while bringing energy prices down is the path that actually helps household budgets.
